Integrating a Palliative Approach: Essentials for Personal Support Workers
This second edition of the text will equip frontline caregivers to respond to the changing needs of any per-
son living with life-limiting illness and their family. You will learn best practice skills for participating fully as a
vital member of the health care team. Integrating a Palliative Approach will increase your confidence and com-
petence in providing compassionate palliative care for the dying person and their family by developing your
capacity to: 1) integrate a palliative approach and support the person and family through decline and during last
days and hours; 2) provide culturally safe palliative care that is free of systemic bias and racism; 3) understand
and provide psychosocial care for the person and family; and 4) develop self-care practices to sustain you. This
is a rare text that engages you with its warmth and heart—it is an essential resource for all frontline caregivers.

Disclaimer
This book is intended only as a resource of general education on the subject matter. Every effort has been made to ensure
the accuracy of the information it contains; however, there is no guarantee that the information will remain current beyond
the date of publication. The information and techniques provided in this book should be used in consultation with qualified
medical health professionals and should not be considered a replacement, substitute, or alternative for their guidance, as-
sessment, or treatment. The author and publisher accept no responsibility or liability with respect to any person or entity for
loss or damage or any other problem caused or alleged to be caused directly or indirectly by information contained in this
book.

DEDICATION
I dedicate this book to personal support workers. I will refer to you in this text as personal support
workers, PSWs, because you support people in such a personal way. I acknowledge that you may
work under a variety of other titles such as: resident care assistant, nurse’s assistant, home support
worker, caregiver, community health worker. Whatever your title, I honour you. I thank you.
For years you have been referred to as the “heart and hands” of the health care team. Now, during
the COVID-19 pandemic, you are being called heroes. Yes, you are, the hearts, hands and heroes of
the health care team. I salute you.
I also dedicate this edition to Ann-Marie Gilbert who led the completion of this text. I thank you.
This resource is dedicated to all the fabulous personal support
workers—the hearts, hands and heroes of the health care team.
v

About the Cover
The arbutus tree shown on the cover of this book is native to southeastern Vancouver Island where
I live, the nearby Gulf Islands, the adjacent coast of mainland British Columbia, and some areas
along the west coast of the United States. Often rooted in the crevices of rock faces along the
shore, this tree, with its gnarled and twisted branches, hangs over the edge and is blown about by
the wind. The smooth, papery bark peels off as new bark grows. Each arbutus is unique.
The arbutus inspires me with its ability to grow, survive, and even thrive in such rough and rocky
areas. This tree graces the cover of my books because to me the arbutus symbolizes the strength of
the human spirit, the amazing ability of human beings to survive, grow, and even thrive in difficult,
harsh, and even traumatic situations. Like the tree, we too get gnarled as we age. And like the tree,
we are all unique.
The arbutus reminds me of those I care for, those who grow in the midst of dying, who grow as
they care for family and friends, and those who grow during their grief and bereavement.
The arbutus reminds me of you, the many personal support workers that I have met over the years.
You also struggle with personal and work challenges, you provide excellent care, you grow in the
midst of challenges, and your stories and lives inspire me.
I wish you well. I hope that you will find great satisfaction and growth in doing this work.
vi Integrating a Palliative Approach: Essentials for Personal Support Workers

ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
We would like to begin by acknowledging that we are on the traditional and unceded
territory of the Coast Salish Peoples, the traditional territories of the W̱ SÁNEĆ First Na-
tions, on whose unceded, non-surrendered traditional territories we work, live and play,
and whose historical relationships with the land continues to this day.
Palliative care is best when provided by team. A palliative care textbook is best when informed and
developed by team.
My gratitude and thanks to these people and groups:
◆ Dr. Antoinette Oberg, researcher, who collaborated on the research to review the 1st Edition, and
the Personal Support Worker (PSW) students and instructors who participated in the research.
The review process informed the writing of the 1st edition and the changes to the 2nd edition.
◆ The palliative care leaders across Canada, those in Nova Scotia, British Columbia, Ontario and
Alberta who developed provincial competency frameworks that identify competencies for
PSWs providing care for people with life-limiting conditions. These competencies served as
guideposts in determining content and updates. Palliative care leaders who reviewed content
or participated in research are Elizabeth Causton, Angela Bruce, Terry Downing, Misha Butot and
Dr. Pippa Hawley.
◆ To the Truth and Reconciliation Commission of Canada for identifying the need to strengthen
Indigenous content in health care resources; the Ontario Palliative Care Network for articulating
competencies for providing care for people who identify as First Nations, Inuit, Métis and urban
Indigenous; Sheila Blackstock, our Gitxsan First Nation advisor, a nurse educator, advocate, story-
teller and writer, who provided wisdom, content, stories, and experiences; Fancy Poitras from the
Mikisew Cree First Nation (Treaty 8) for her thorough review of the 1st edition and for many prac-
tical suggestions; and Estrella Racoma Whetung, from the Mississauga Nishnaabe & Lucbanin
nations for the feather icon that she created to identify cultural content within the text.
◆ PSW Jackie McDonald, and PSW Instructors Zola Goebel and Meena Singh for insight into the
daily practices and challenges of PSWs providing palliative care and integrating a palliative ap-
proach to care.
◆ Dr. Kelli Stajduhar, Felicia Kontopidis, and Rami Shami, for identifying and advocating for those
who are underserved by palliative care, and who advocate for a trauma-informed approach in
palliative care.
◆ Content contributors: Elizabeth Causton contributed to the psychosocial content; Andrea War-
nick contributed to the section on supporting children; Cari Hoffman contributed to the section
on advance care planning; David K. Wright provided the content on Medical Assistance in Dying;
and Françoise Mathieu contributed to the chapter on self-care.
vii

◆ Editor Sarah Weber and graphic designer Greg Glover for your incredible work and diligence
in helping to create a resource that is easy to read, engaging, or, as I like to say, “delicious and
digestible.”
The Life and Death Matters team who are equally committed to improving palliative care through
education and resource development, Ann-Marie Gilbert and Ted Murray. Ann-Marie brings skills
and passion as a writer, editor, scientist, researcher, educator, computer software technologist and
family caregiver. While completing this edition, our son Geordie Murray, was killed in an accident.
Ann-Marie, educated in grief and bereavement from the work that she has done with Life and
Death Matters, understood the need for me to have time to grieve, and she became the lead
for completing this text. Ted Murray, my dear and fabulous husband and my partner in Life and
Death Matters is an ongoing source of inspiration and support—and he is practically speaking,
irreplaceable.
Finally, and most importantly, I thank you—PSWs. I am inspired by you—I esteem you. This book is
for you. Thank you for sharing yourselves and your stories and thank you for teaching me.

Contents xiii

xiv Integrating a Palliative Approach: Essentials for Personal Support Workers

PREFACE
I came to hospice nursing as a child.
A few of my earliest recollections of death include a dead rat and a lovely transparent dead leaf.
I tried to nurse both back to life, with no success. When my siblings and I found a dead bird, we
decided we were not doing well in the healing department and opted for burial.
During my teenage years, I lived with my aunt Frankie. Frankie, a nurse, was the master family care-
giver. During my teens she cared for many family members and friends through aging, illness, and
dying. I learned that death is a part of life.
As a teen and young adult, I had friends who died. I learned that even the young die. I learned
that people die from cancer, accidents, and suicide. I learned that when people are in need, you
respond.
When I was in my 20s, my uncle and my father died. My uncle died after months of excruciating
pain, not able to access morphine because, he was told, it needed to be saved for when the pain
was “really bad,” and because “he might become addicted.” My father died with congestion in his
lungs, gasping for air. No medications were available to relieve his breathlessness or decrease his
congestion. I learned that dying can be painful.
In the late 1980s, as the hospice palliative care movement spread across North America, I began
nursing at Victoria Hospice. I learned about symptoms and symptom management. I learned
about loss and grief, last days and hours, rituals, transitions, and traditions. I learned about people,
about families, about communication. I became more comfortable talking about dying, grief, loss,
and death. I learned that symptoms can often be managed, and that death can be less painful
when palliative care principles and practices are integrated in care. I also learned that care is best
provided by team.
In this second edition of the text, I have aimed to ensure that content is current and addresses
the palliative care competencies identified by provincial leaders in Nova Scotia, British Columbia,
Ontario and Alberta and the Ontario, in particular, the competencies for caring for the First Nation,
Inuit, Metis and urban Indigenous people. Given the statistics on those who have experienced or
are experiencing trauma, I also integrated a trauma informed approach to care throughout the
text.
If you are a Personal Support Worker or a caregiver, it is my sincere hope that this resource and
the companion workbook will help you be more competent and feel more confident in providing
excellent compassionate care for the dying person1 and their family. I hope that you will be able to
serve as a leader in your community in strengthening the integration of a palliative approach in all
care settings.
1 In this text, a person who is dying is referred to as “the dying person” or “the person,” and the people the dying person
has identified as family, including biological, and chosen family, friends and all significant others, are referred to as
“family.”
xv

If you are an educator, I hope that this text, the companion workbook and other teaching/learning
materials will help you to educate Personal Support Workers, and help you prepare them to pro-
vide excellent, compassionate, competent care.
I wish you well in this learning and invite you to share with me your experiences via the contact link
on the Life and Death Matters website (www.lifeanddeathmatters.ca).
Kath Murray
Life and Death Matters
2020
xvi Integrating a Palliative Approach: Essentials for Personal Support Workers

Starting the Journey—How to Use This Book
Welcome to the second edition of Integrating a Palliative Approach: Essentials for Personal Support
Workers. We’re happy to be here with you, supporting you as you learn the skills, attitudes and
knowledge for providing compassionate palliative and end-of-life care.
Whew! That’s a mouthful isn’t it? However, you have nothing to fear! This set of resources is de-
signed with you, the learner, at the forefront. Designed and written to be interesting and easy-to-
understand. Knowing your course load when in school, and the workload when providing care,
we knew we needed resources that captivated our learners. We hope that you enjoy your learning
experience.
Let me tell you what is new in this text.
Touchstones: Through the text are “touchstones” which identify competencies. Use the
touchstones to help you to understand the key concepts for providing palliative and end-of-
life care.
Best practices: Develop best practices to ensure your success as a caregiver, in any setting, to
any age group. You can apply the best practices in your practice every day.
Cultural Safety: Embedded in the text are key strategies to help you communicate
with and provide care respectfully for any person from any culture. Based on Canada’s
Truth and Reconciliation Commission Calls to Action, learning to provide culturally safe care
with a trauma-informed approach will help you provide excellent care for any person and their
family. The feather icon will draw your attention to cultural considerations. The feather was
chosen because birds often hold a role cross-culturally as important intermediaries, messen-
gers, and guides for knowledge.
So take a deep breath in—and breathe out slowly. We’ll get through this together.
If you are a PSW student, use the companion workbook as a guide for studying the textbook.
The workbook builds on the basics. Do not feel you need to learn everything right now. Follow
your instructor’s directions about what content you need to know at this point, and what you can
put aside to learn in the coming months or years.
If you are a PSW already working in the field, you may want to read this book cover to cover,
use it as a reference as you provide care for people and as questions arise, or share it with people
whose loved ones are dying.
I believe that teaching is an invitation to learn and learning is an invitation to teach. As you learn,
share your learning with your colleagues. As you share your learning, you will not only help them
learn the importance of integrating a palliative approach into caregiving, but also deepen your
own knowledge..

Yetta’s experience2 (see below) of a steady decline highlights
the opportunities to prepare when death is anticipated and
the ways to support person-centred care. Yetta (my mother)

what we called the “death benefits,” including the opportun-
ity for friends to gather and her ability to choose what she
wanted to do with her limited energy and time. She regis-
tered with the provincial palliative care program. Her friends
and community supported her by bringing food and offer-
ing assistance. As a family, we cared for her and prepared

For some people, the speed of the steady decline may be Stuttering decline
challenging. It may feel too fast, and they may feel they have
no time to prepare for or adjust to the changes. The decline Figure 3 . A stuttering decline
may feel particularly fast if the team does not explain to
the person and the family that the person is dying, or if the
team explains but the person or family is not ready or able
to process the information. The decline may also feel too fast
if there are great differences in how individual family mem-
bers hear and act on the information received.
Maggie’s Experience—a Steady Decline
Maggie is a respected Indigenous3 Elder living in a remote
community in northern British Columbia. Community mem-
Time ≈ years
bers rely on Maggie’s knowledge of traditional medicine to Death
treat family and friends. She is a residential school survivor
and provided her testimony to the Truth and Reconciliation
Commission4 to help her community heal. Through role mod-
elling and living a traditional healthy lifestyle, she is learning
the culture and traditional language of her community and
teaching it to others.
Maggie is a breast cancer survivor of 22 years. Her health
declined recently—she lost six kilograms in one month and
began experiencing lower back pain and nausea. Com-
munity members took Maggie to the small-town hospital
95 kilometres away; however, she was soon transferred to a
regional hospital four hours away for emergency explora-
tory surgery. Maggie awoke from surgery to find she had an
ostomy on her lower abdomen. The surgeon gently explained
that she had stage 4 ovarian cancer with secondary sites and
had about four months to live.
Maggie wishes to go home to die.5 In her culture, family and
community perform sacred ceremonies when a person is
dying. But the health care team would not be able to support
her adequately at home. Instead, she was transferred to the
local hospital and settled in a private room.
Maggie feels isolated and alone. Her person is supported but
she needs the spiritual and emotional support of family and
community for a good death.
3 The term “Indigenous” will be used to refer to all First Nations, Inuit, Métis and urban Indigenous people.

ments, reports, and calls to action, and can be accessed at https://nctr.ca/map.php.
5 In Indigenous culture worldwide, a person is believed to be a spirit having a human experience. When a person dies, sacred ceremonies help the spirit
journey back to its home (Anderson & Woticky, 2018).

The stuttering decline (Figure 3) occurs over multiple years
and describes the decline of people with chronic, life-l imiting
illnesses leading to organ failure such as congestive heart fail-
ure, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, end-stage kidney
disease, autoimmune dieases such as HIV, and comorbidities
such as Parkinson’s disease or dementia-related illnesses. This
pattern alternates between periods of substantial decline
and periods of partial recovery. A period of decline may result
from a worsening of a chronic or life-limiting illness, or from
a fall, a fracture, the flu, pneumonia, and so on. On occasion,
the person may require hospitalization and treatment, or they
may require an adjustment of medications to help stabilize
their condition. Following treatment and stabilization, the
person may recover most or some of their previous level of
functioning and may again enjoy some of the activities they
used to do. The person may be stable for weeks or months
and then experience another substantial period of decline
when their life-limiting illness changes or they experience
another infection, fall, or flu. Each period of decline contrib-
utes to an overall decline. The last three months often show
the highest rate of decline. Eventually, the person is not able
to recover from a period of decline and dies.
The stuttering decline can feel ambiguous for the family
because the person may decline and recover repeatedly.
Because of the multiple declines and recoveries the person

Caring for the Dying—Like Running a Race
Caring for the dying is like running a race in which the distance We now realize we are in a marathon. No one trained for this.
keeps changing. Who takes responsibility for the food and water station? Who
stands at the tricky corners to make sure we don’t get lost?
At the time of my aunt’s diagnosis, we were convinced she had a
very short prognosis. We thought we were in a 100-metre sprint. As hospice workers, we are the ones at the tricky corners. We need
to know how hard it is, how confusing and exhausting when
Everyone came, called, sent letters and flowers, and declared the finish line moves. Imagine having 2 kilometres to go in a 10-
love. Nobody was thinking about pacing. kilometre event and then seeing a new sign that says, “Only 10
kilometres to go.” How does one continue?
Then things changed. There was hope and a treatment plan
— Michelle Dale, MSW, RSW
offered. It became clear that this was not a sprint, but possibly a
10-kilometre event. We needed to pace ourselves.
Then there was surgery, and that was awfully hard, and every-
one moved in again. Maybe it was a 5-kilometre event? We
needed to give everything, but also save a little for the finish.
Now she is doing well again with an uncertain prognosis and
talk of survival.

and they have no time to prepare. When dying takes a few
hours or days after the initial incident, such as with a stroke
or pandemic infection, the family will be forced to make
difficult decisions immediately about their loved one’s
care. Because the dying and death of their loved one are
unexpected, the family may feel unprepared and struggle
with decision making. People who have experienced the
sudden death of a loved one may grieve not having had
time to prepare, to anticipate death, or to say goodbye. They
may compare their experience to being hit by a truck: you
do not see the truck coming, are not able to brace yourself
and prepare for the hit, and are left reeling, wondering what
just happened.
Some people say that a good death for them would be a
sudden death, but that a sudden death would be a bad
death for the people left behind, and for those who need to

Integrating a Palliative Approach to Care and death.
A palliative approach is an approach that aims to reduce
suffering by integrating the principles, practices, and phil-
osophy of palliative care into the care of people with any
life- limiting or serious illness early in the disease process,
across all care settings.
This approach acknowledges that care of the dying is the
responsibility of every member of the team and that spe-
cialized hospice and palliative care services are available
through referrals as needed.

Summary
In your practice as a PSW, remember that dying is a natural
process and that the dying person and family are the unit of
care. Reflect on the pattern of decline the person is experi-
encing and the challenges associated with that decline.
Support the person and family by using key strategies for
integrating a palliative approach and providing palliative
care. Gather and communicate information about the per-
son and changes in their condition to the team, and, as a
vital member of the team, participate in care planning with
the interprofessional team.

Preparing to Care
Dying Is a Journey—Supporting the Person on Their Journey
People may refer to the process of dying as a journey, a final
journey, or the journey at the end of life. For an Indigenous I learned as a child that I needed to stop, look, and listen to be
person with traditional beliefs, it may be considered the safe when crossing the street. Stop, look both ways, and listen
journey of the spirit back to the spirit world (Ian Anderson for oncoming cars before I crossed the street.
Continuing Education Program in End-of-Life Care, 2017).
This wisdom applies to you as a PSW when caring for a dying
You are invited to consider the process of dying as a journey person and their family.
to understand how to prepare, as a personal support worker
When you stop, you put your agenda and needs on hold.
(PSW), to provide care for the dying person and family. In
person-centred care, the journey is that of the dying person
When you look, you look at the person, their physical pres-
and family—it is not your journey. As a member of the team
ence, their surroundings, their family.
providing health care, you are a companion who provides
care and support for the dying person and family on their
When you listen, you hear what they say and consider what
journey.
might be felt but not said.
When you stop, look, and listen, then sometimes you are
invited to cross the street and journey with the dying person.
At some point, the person moves on alone on their journey,
and you remain behind. Your life is changed. And you some-
times feel that you have been taught by “the masters.”
Preparing for the Journey by Sorting Your Baggage
To accompany a dying person and family on their
journey, you will need to be aware of your bag-
gage—the values, beliefs, and opinions that you have
accumulated throughout your life. Baggage includes cul-
tural and religious practices, as well as stereotypes, prejudi-
ces, biases, and hopes and dreams (see Figure 1, first illustra-
tion). As human beings, we all have baggage. What is
important, however, is that you are aware of your baggage,
sort it, and set it aside when preparing to provide per-
son-centred care for a dying person and family.
For example, you may have an opinion about what a “good
death” would be for you or beliefs about how people should
prepare for death. It is natural for you to have these opin-
ions and beliefs. But if you are not aware of them and do
not consciously set them aside before providing care, you
may find yourself encouraging a person or their family to do

something that feels right to you, regardless of whether or

Strategies for Developing Self-Awareness For the health care team, supporting an appropriate death
means addressing the person’s definition of a good death
Many practices are available for learning to sort your bag- and facilitating those characteristics that are possible. For
gage and develop self-awareness. One strategy is to adopt example, many people desire to die a pain-free death. As a
a daily reflective practice—writing about a belief or value, PSW, you could support an appropriate death by observing
considering the origin, and reflecting on how the belief or the person for signs of pain, and recording and reporting
value has served you in your life and how it has affected your these signs to the nurse.
interactions with other people. Considering different pieces
of baggage individually through reflective writing can help
Figure 2 . Characteristics of a good death and a bad death
you understand your motivations—biases, needs, wants,
hopes, and dreams—and allow you to release or put aside
a piece of baggage when providing care. The companion
workbook to this textbook provides a wide range of reflect-
ive activities for developing self-awareness.
Self-awareness can build as you develop humility by asking
for and accepting feedback, openly sharing what you don’t
know, examining and addressing your biases, and asking
questions and listening deeply. Commit to lifelong learning
to build self-awareness, because learning brings new experi-
ences that foster self-awareness. Other strategies that can
develop self-awareness include individual or group coun-
selling, and self-exploration experiences such as meditation
and mindfulness practices. Choose a practice that works for
you, and commit to the practice to build self-awareness and
to help you sort and understand your baggage.
Supporting an Appropriate
Death
The journey of dying is vitally important for both the per-
son who is dying and the people who will live with the
memories of that person. There are no dress rehearsals for
dying. It is your role as a member of the health care team to
support a dying person to live and die in a way that aligns
with who they are and how they have lived—to support an Reflective Activity
appropriate or “personal” death.
One component of person-centred care is learning about
the person’s definition of a good death and a bad death.

their last days and hours. You support an appropriate death

keep your definition of a good or bad death separate from death, as much as is realistic and possible.
the dying person’s definition.

The staff phoned to tell me that Mom was declining, and I As she was dying, over 100 visitors found a safe area for their

When I heard about an “appropriate death,” I remembered
that she was a very private person. Mom had always pro-
tected us from seeing or experiencing difficult things. When
I remembered that, I thought, “Wasn’t that just like her to die
when I wasn’t there!” With this new understanding, I felt more
at peace with her death.
In addition to being aware of your values, beliefs, and opin-
ions, knowing those of the dying person will enable you to
support the dying person to experience what they define as
an appropriate death.

empathy is putting yourself in the other person’s place by

Committing to Leadership open to conversations on death, dying, and palliative care.
Using the tools in Chapter 3, “Using Standardized Tools,” may
Anyone can be a great leader, regardless of their role, title, help you find your voice, express concerns, and share appro-
or background. Leadership is the process whereby you posi- priate information with the care team.
tively influence another person’s abilities to meet their goals.
Strong leadership is particularly important in palliative care,
Reflective Activity
where the most vulnerable individuals depend on HCPs,

Reflect on the goal of the preceding competency and
consider your opportunities and goals for leadership as a
PSW.
Competency Touchstone
The PSW:
Acts as a mentor and coach for HCA peers in
all domains.
(BC Centre for Palliative Care, 2018)
PSWs demonstrate their leadership abilities in the way
they present themselves and interact with people in
their work and personal life. Leading by example—that
is, role- modelling ways of being in their interactions with
members of the team, the dying person, and family—dem-
onstrates leadership. Demonstrating best practices, for
example, respecting the dying person and family, putting
aside baggage, and so on, encourages people around you
to follow the same practices. By virtue of their role, PSWs
show leadership by supporting others in a manner that is
non-judgmental, empathetic, collaborative, and focused on
the whole person and family. The team members rely on
PSWs to report information and insights that can guide the
overall direction of care. And then, PSWs lead by integrat-
ing the expertise of other team members into the care they
provide by following the care plan.

and family with questions. Recognizing the balance requires to answer questions, you will need to ask questions gently
listening to the person and family, and developing experi- to gather information and inform the team.
ence in these situations, and may also be partially intuitive.
The goal is to ensure that the person and family feel sup-
ported as they share and receive information, and make care
decisions.

Chapter 3: Using Standardized Tools 59

Edmonton Symptom Assessment System (ESAS)
The Edmonton Symptom Assessment System (ESAS) (Bruera
et al., 1991; Watanabe et al., 2011) shown in Figure 4 provides a
“snapshot” of the person’s perception of nine symptoms and
other problems they may be experiencing. It is a practical
tool that enables a rapid self-evaluation of symptoms, using
a horizontal visual scale from 0 to 10, in which 0 means the
symptom is absent, 1 means the symptom causes minimal
distress, and 10 means the symptom is the worst possible.
While the tool was developed originally for use with people
being treated for cancer, it is currently used across Can-
ada and the United States for people with any life-limiting
illnesses.
Instructions: The person experiencing symptoms needs to
provide answers to the questions in the ESAS, but the family
or an HCP can record the answers. The person can use the
body map in the ESAS to indicate where they feel the dis-
comfort or the symptom. When checking for changes in a
symptom, for example, after a medication change, it is help-
ful if the time between evaluations is consistent. It is also
helpful if the person considers the time when answering the
questions (e.g., the present moment, the last 8 hours, the
last 24 hours). The ESAS cannot be used when a person is no
longer able to self-evaluate.
When the person indicates that a symptom has worsened
since the previous evaluation or that a new symptom has
developed, share this information with the nurse or team. A
team member can follow up with a thorough assessment.

Pain Assessment Checklist for Seniors with Limited Ability to Communicate
(PACSLAC-II)
The PACSLAC-II1 tool (Figure 7) provides a checklist of pain
behaviours that may indicate pain and can be used when
gathering information about pain experienced by a person
who is not able to communicate about their pain (Chan et
al., 2014). This checklist may help to identify behaviours that
indicate a person is experiencing pain.
Instructions: Develop a habit of observing each person
regularly to identify changes in their behaviours. Observe
the person for the behaviours listed in the PACSLAC-II dur-
ing an activity such as when the person is being transferred.
Record on the checklist the presence of behaviours that
might indicate pain. You might compare the noted behav-
iours with previous observations. Record and report chan-
ges in behaviour, as well as when behaviours do not change.
1 COPYRIGHT: The PACSLAC-II is copyrighted by Sarah Chan, Thomas Hadjistavropoulos and Shannon Fuchs-Lacelle. Reprinted with permission.
The developers of the PACSLAC-II specifically disclaim any liability arising directly or indirectly from use or application of the PACSLAC-II. Use of the
PACSLAC-II may not be appropriate for some patients and the PACSLAC-II is not a substitute for a thorough assessment by a qualified health professional.
The PACSLAC-II (like other observational pain assessment tools) is a screening tool and not a definitive indicator of pain. As such, sometimes it may fail
to identify pain and other times, it may incorrectly signal the presence of pain. The PACSALC-II should be used by qualified health care staff within the
context of their broader knowledge and examination of the patient..

The SBAR Communication Tool for PSWs
The SBAR Communication Tool was designed in the airline
industry to increase the safety of flying (Doucette, 2006).
The tool ensures consistent information is included in every
report, helping to eliminate confusion and errors. A modified
version of the SBAR tool effectively improved communica-
tions in health care between the nurse and physician, which
has led to the widespread use of the tool by all members of
the team (Institute for Healthcare Improvement, 2017).
SBAR is an acronym for “situation,” “background,” “assessment,”
and “request/recommendation,” as indicated in Figure 9.

Medications for Pain Relief
Medications used to relieve pain are divided into three cat- • Antidepressant medications (e.g., nortriptyline) can be
egories, based on their function: non-opioid analgesics, effective in decreasing neuropathic pain if used at low
adjuvant analgesics, and opioid2 analgesics. Sometimes doses.
the person will have better pain management when they • Antibiotics can reduce pain by treating infections. For
receive medications from each category at the same time. example, treating a bladder infection with an antibiotic
can decrease pain felt when voiding.
Non-Opioid Analgesics
Opioid Analgesics
Non-opioid analgesics may be used to relieve pain, fever, or
inflammation. Acetaminophen (Tylenol) is an example of a Opioids are key medications for relieving severe pain, which
commonly used analgesic. Mild pain can often be treated they do by decreasing the number and strength of pain
using regular doses of acetaminophen. signals reaching the brain. Opioids are also essential for
managing severe breathlessness and coughing. Commonly
Anti-inflammatory medications (e.g., ibuprofen) decrease used opioids in palliative care are morphine, sufen tanil, and
local tissue inflammation, which in turn decreases the fre- fentanyl.
quency and intensity of pain signals. Sometimes people
find that a combination of non-opioid analgesics and anti-
inflammatory medications is more effective than either
alone, and simultaneous use of them is safe. If pain increases,
it may be helpful to add an adjuvant analgesic or an opioid.
Adjuvant Analgesics
The word “adjuvant” means “helpful” or “a helpful person or
thing.” Adjuvants are medications that have a primary pur-
pose to treat one problem (e.g., depression, seizures) but
also relieve pain, as a secondary function. Below are some
examples of adjuvant medications:
• Steroid medications (e.g., prednisone, dexamethasone)
decrease inflammation and swelling around a tumour.
This relieves pressure on tissues and consequently
decreases pain. Steroids can prevent or decrease
post-radiation pain caused by swelling in the radiated
area, or headache from brain tumours.
• Anticonvulsant medications (e.g., gabapentin) work
by decreasing the excitability of nerves and thereby
decreasing the number of pain signals sent to the
brain. They are often used to decrease neuropathic
(nerve) pain.
• Antispasmodics (e.g., atropine) relieve pain by decreas-
ing muscle spasms. These medications are useful for
bladder spasms or intestinal spasms.
2 Currently, health care professionals refer to these medications as “opioids” rather than “narcotics.” The term “narcotics” is commonly used to refer to illegal
street drugs and also to drugs that induce sleep, and therefore the term is inappropriate in the context of palliative care.

Fear of Being Judged
In some cultures, being stoic when experiencing pain is
an important characteristic. This may lead some people to
refuse pain medications, including opioids, to avoid being
judged as “weak” when they experience significant pain
(Narayan, 2010). Other people may fear they will not receive
appropriate pain relief with opioids because of their hist-
ory of drug or opioid addiction, the effects of stereotypes,
or past interactions in health care where individuals were
labelled as “drug seeking.” PSWs who address their personal
feelings and avoid judging will be prepared to provide cul-
turally safe, unbiased palliative care for the dying person and
family.

Other side effects include a dry sore mouth, itchiness, urin-
ary retention or hesitancy, sweating, and muscle twitching
or jerking.

Supporting Physical Comfort with Traditional Healing and
Medicines
Traditional healing and medicine practices support
the holistic characteristics of palliative care because Cultural Touchstone
they consider all parts of the person. Among Indigenous The PSW:
people around the world, providing options for traditional Recognizes the importance of Traditional
Medicine particularly when caring for First
healing and medicines alongside Western medical treat-
Nations, Inuit, Métis and urban Indigenous
ments is reported to benefit the person spiritually, emotion-
people.
ally, and culturally (Gall et al., 2018).
(Ontario Palliative Care Network, 2019)
Your openness to and awareness of traditional healing and
medicines can determine whether a person or family feels
comfortable suggesting using them. Avoid judging. Instead,
be curious about the person and their culture. Learn about
their traditional practices and, with the person’s permission,
share information about these practices with the team to
consider including as a component of providing care.
The traditional healing and medicines of Indigenous people
vary, depending on the dying person’s community. Provid-
ing for cultural safety means asking a person about their
preferences for traditional healing and medicines. You can-
not assume that all Indigenous people will want traditional
medicines as part of their care, nor that all people from a
specific culture will have the same preferences for trad-
itional healing and medicines.
For some people, the close relationship developed with the
provider of traditional healing and medicines makes this
care more familiar and comfortable than Western medicine
(Auger et al., 2016). When a person is interested in receiving
traditional healing and medicines, record and report this
to the nurse and team so that they can seek out providers
within the person’s community. The team will need to col-
laborate with the provider of traditional healing and medi-
cines to prevent unwanted interactions between Western
and traditional medicine.
If the person is located far away from their home, the team
might consider alternative ways to connect the person with
their traditional providers, for example, using internet-based
video conferencing for non-touch-based healing.

Providing Comfort with Music: Room 2173
When I was growing up, my Sunday afternoon pastime was
making music with my dad. He would be at the organ and I
would be at the piano. Music drew us together. When Dad
had his first and second quadruple bypasses, it was no sur-
prise that music played a role in his recovery. The tunes ener-
gized him and gave him hope for recovery.
The diagnosis of stage 4 non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma was as
unexpected as a snowfall in July. I remember one morning
in those first months like it was yesterday. He sat in the rock-
ing chair and I sat on his organ bench. Intuitively, we knew
Reflexology our exchange would be about those songs we had played
for years. Our tears sang the words that day. Several weeks
During a reflexology treatment, the person’s hands, feet, before Dad died, and just one week before Christmas, I played
and ears are massaged to stimulate reflex points that corres- him familiar carols. These tunes evoked the memories we
pond to a specific area or organ of the body. Reflexology is shared and the hope of one last celebration together. The last
based on the Chinese concept of qu (pronounced “chee”), or hours with Dad are still etched in my heart. In Room 217 at the
energy. Massaging reflex points helps qu to flow freely, and Uxbridge Cottage Hospital, my five siblings, Mom, and I sur-
reportedly reduces tension and improves circulation and rounded his bed singing the songs he loved. Dad tried singing
healing (Reflexology Association of Canada, 2017). along. Music companioned Dad in his final transition. It was
a gift, wrapped with the ribbon of release for all of us.
— Bev Foster

I hear that you and Sam used to sing together.
Would you consider singing for him now?
• Be open to adding new tools to your comfort basket.
• Invite the dying person to teach you new comfort
measures.
When working with family, consider the following as ways to
provide comfort and support:
• Invite the family to participate in providing comfort
measures.
• Provide the family with information about the com-
fort measures you use and how the family can also use
them when providing care.
• Encourage the family to think of things the person used
to do that brought them comfort. Consider whether
those things could be adapted to be comfort measures
now.
• Provide opportunities for intimacy. Keep in mind that
family members might want to lie next to or snuggle
with the person or want time for intimacy. They may
want permission to lie on the bed. Side rails can pre-
vent people from falling out of bed. Closing the door
can provide some privacy.
• Invite family and friends to share their comfort meas-
ures, if appropriate.
I understand that you studied therapeutic touch
together. Have you thought of offering a treatment
to Bob?
• Create your own comfort basket. Be creative with com-
fort measures!
It is important to choose comfort measures that will best
meet the needs and preferences of the person who has a
particular symptom at a particular time. The illustration on
the previous page might inspire you!

Part 2 of this chapter presents the common symptoms that addressed by the team.
a person living and dying with life-limiting illness might
experience through a steady, stuttering, or slow decline.
While these symptoms are common, not every person will
experience every symptom.
As you gather information and provide comfort measures,
remember to do the following:
• Ask the Surprise Question regularly . When provid-
ing care, keep in mind that the person has life-limiting
illness(es) and will die sometime in the future. Regularly
asking yourself the Surprise Question—”Would I be
surprised if this [person] died in the next 12 months?”—
when providing care can help you to notice changes
that may indicate the person’s decline has shifted
toward death. Depending on the person, you may
want to ask the Surprise Question once a month, or
after they experience an illness, a fall, or another kind
of incident. Record and report your reflections to the
team so it can benefit from your insight gained while
providing direct care daily.
Sharing this information with the team can help the
team to prepare to meet the person’s and family’s
care needs through the last months, days, and hours.
Given the uncertainties associated with the stuttering
and slow declines, it may be helpful to begin prepar-
ing people early on for their loved one’s dying. With
dementia and many life-limiting illnesses, the person
may be thought to have six months left to live and then
die the next week.

Medications and Treatments
Unfortunately, no medications or specific care strategies are
available that prevent or cure anorexia or cachexia. There-
fore, all treatments are supportive only and do not provide
long-term solutions.
A few medications stimulate the appetite but are effective
only if the person can digest food. A combination of medi-
cations may help the person gain weight in the short term.

106 Integrating a Palliative Approach: Essentials for Personal Support Workers

Rehydration
In some situations, dehydration can be treated by rehy-
drating the person, either through intravenous (IV) fluids or
by a less invasive procedure wherein fluids from an IV bag
enter the fatty tissue under the skin through a needle (hypo-
dermoclysis). This fluid is absorbed slowly into the circula-
tory system and distributed throughout the body. Fatty
open areas, such as the abdomen, thigh, upper back, or arm,
are used for this type of rehydration; however, the location
of the needle that delivers the fluid is determined by the
person’s comfort. This subcutaneous route, which is easier to
set up and less invasive than the IV route, may be preferred.
Competency Touchstone
The PSW:
Identifies situations potentially involving
conflict and collaborates with the inter-
professional team to resolve.
(BC Centre for Palliative Care, 2018)
Chapter 4: Supporting Physical Comfort | Dehydration 107

Delirium
In order for me (the brain) to work, the rest of my body parts and
organs have to work. If they don’t do their job, then I can’t do
mine! When my body does not work well, then I get confused: I
can’t find the right words, pay attention, make clear decisions,
remember things, or sort out what is past, present, or future!
Sometimes I hallucinate and often I misunderstand. And worst
of all, no one seems to understand me. Sometimes I am sleepy
when I am supposed to be awake, and other times I am awake
when everyone else is trying to sleep. Sometimes I get agitated
and upset, and other times I am just sleepy and wonder what
the fuss is all around me. Sometimes I see things that no one else
sees. Sometimes the things I see make sense (like the time I mis-
took the nurse for my daughter, or the time I thought the bells
were the fire alarm when my house burned down), but other
times, the images that I see are scary, and I am scared.
I understand that what I experience is called “delirium.” When I
am delirious, I can’t always answer questions about it, but then,
a little while later, I might be clear enough to tell you my mem-
ories. In fact, we might even be able to find themes and reasons
for the delirium.
When I ask the nurse why delirium occurs, I am told a whole
list of things. But one thing that no one mentions is dying. Did
you know that most of the causes of delirium occur naturally in
the dying process? And in addition to the things within my body The toxins are building up. My immune system is weak. I get
that cause delirium, medications are a really common cause of more infections—urinary tract infections and pneumonia are
it, and my body and I, we often need some of these medications my constant companions. In order to do their job well, the anti-
that cause delirium in order to manage common symptoms of biotics need a healthy immune system, so in my body they can’t
dying. So, as I see it, dying causes delirium. do their job well. I expect that at some point the antibiotics will
stop working altogether!
Let me tell you why …
Oh, and my bladder and bowels aren’t working well, and with
My body and I, we are connected. My body is sick right now. the side effects of medications, the bowels get constipated. Let
As the disease progresses through my body, my desire for food me tell you, when the bowel is constipated, I can’t see straight!
and my ability to swallow and digest food decrease. My organs,
including my kidneys, liver, and heart, are not functioning well. The long and short of it is, you can look at the list of all the dif-
My metabolism is changing. I am low on water and oxygen, ferent things that cause delirium, but dying … well, dying is the
and my electrolytes are out of balance. “perfect storm” for delirium.

As a PSW you can help prevent delirium in the following
ways:
• Provide a quiet, familiar, safe environment with adequate
lighting.
• Maintain the person’s daily routines as much as possible.
• If the person is receiving opioids and is able to swallow,
offer liquids frequently to help their body get rid of the
byproducts of the medication.

Medications and Treatments
Medications can help manage symptoms of delirium, such
as paranoia, hallucinations, and insomnia. The person’s goals
of care will direct the physician, team, and family when they
make decisions about medications. The care plans in these
two scenarios would be different:
My dad just wants to be comfortable. He does not want
any further tests or treatments.
Dad was clear-headed this morning and he wants to be
able to attend the anniversary party this weekend. I think
it is important to him for this delirium to be investigated
and treated if possible.

Chapter 4: Supporting Physical Comfort | Depression 115

For the Family
Depression is, by its nature, a very lonely and isolating
experience. Family may struggle to understand the loved
one’s experience with depression and may feel helpless as
they search for ways to connect and support their loved
one. Friends and family may benefit from having their own
support network.
Medications and Treatments
Treatments available for depression include counselling and
cognitive therapy, as well as many medication options. The
physician/nurse practitioner will prescribe the person medi-
cations as needed. The period of time (up to six weeks) of
waiting for such medications to be effective can be difficult,
and during it the person may be at increased risk of self-
harm. You can support the person by connecting with them
regularly through this period while they wait for the medi-
cation to take effect.
The person may want to consider counselling or spirit-
ual support to help them heal. Provide information about
counselling resources in accordance with the policy for your
facility and location.
Reinforce to the person and family the importance of
reporting all symptoms that are causing distress, physical or
psychological, as both may influence well-being.

In the moment
A person experiencing difficult breathing may be anxious,
fearful, and confused. You can support them by remaining
calm and staying with them while calling for the nurse. The
following strategies can support comfort (Mayo Clinic Staff,
2017; Smothers & Buck, 2012):
• Use a fan to cool the face or open a window for fresh air.
• Consider lowering the temperature in the room.
• Provide humidified air.

Care, 2017). A PSW would suspect fatigue if the person
(BC Centre for Palliative Care, 2018)
• Couldn’t complete their activities of daily living and
other usual tasks
• Slept or rested for more time than usual but arose with-
out feeling refreshed or energized
• Said things like “I am still so tired” or “I slept all day, and I
could sleep all night”
• Started a task but was not able to complete it
• Felt distressed after engaging in even limited activities
• Withdrew from or lost interest in activities
• Refused to see visitors
Using the Symptom Framework for PSWs—fatigue
If you observe signs of fatigue in the person you are car-
ing for, use the Symptom Framework for PSWs adapted
for fatigue (Figure 8) to explore your observations. Choose
a time to ask questions when the person is freshest and
most energetic. Always proceed with a trauma-informed
approach. As you ask questions, remember to be open to
different understandings of fatigue by people from different

122 Integrating a Palliative Approach: Essentials for Personal Support Workers

When appropriate, invite the family to help the person with
activities of daily living. Look for opportunities to share infor-
mation with the family about how to
• Help their loved one without “doing it all” for them
• Be open to adapting plans daily or hourly, depending
on how the person is feeling
• Be mindful, when visiting the person, of their energy
and needs
• Revise the care plan with the nurse as determined by
the person’s needs
• Ask the nurse for more information about fatigue
You can also help the family by reinforcing the following
messages provided by the team:
• Drowsiness is a normal side effect when a person begins
to take opioids or when the opioid dose increases. The
person’s drowsiness will fade after a few days.
• As death nears, it is normal and expected that people
become weaker and sleep more.
Medications and Treatments
The person benefits most when treatments remove the
cause of fatigue (e.g., antibiotics to remove an infection,
blood transfusion to treat anemia). When it is not possible
to remove the cause of fatigue, depending on its cause a
short course of steroids can boost the person’s energy. Con-
sider this option when a person wants to participate in cer-
tain activities (e.g., daily activities, a special event such as a
wedding), and discontinue the steroid medication when the
person cannot get out of bed.

When a person cannot manage their mouth care, you can
use these strategies: Medications and Treatments
• Moisten their oral mucosa gently using packaged or
homemade mouth swabs. Medications may be necessary for pain relief, to prevent
• Use a fine-mist spray bottle to moisten the oral mucosa or treat infections, and to encourage healing. Topical
with water. treatments may be “swish and swallow” or “swish and spit”
• Offer these liquids for rinsing their mouth: depending on the medication. Report to the nurse when
• Water a person is not able to swallow or spit. Depending on your
• Salt water (1 teaspoon table salt mixed into 2 cups scope of practice, you may be directed to “paint” medication
boiled and cooled water) on with mouth swabs or to squirt medication onto the inner
• Alcohol-free germicidal mouthwash diluted to half cheeks using a syringe.
strength with water
• Brandy diluted with water to remove crusts and
reduce breath odour
• Club soda to loosen materials on oral mucosa and
teeth.
• Apply lubricant to the oral mucosa using a swab, after
the mouth has been cleaned and rinsed.
• Apply lip balm after applying the lubricant (use water-
based products if the person is receiving oxygen).
For the family
Invite the family to participate in mouth care as one way
to support their loved one. Remind them to use gloves if
the person has a thrush infection. Family can participate
by using the fine-mist spray bottle to moisten the person’s
mouth.
Irene was in her last days of life. She was not eating and could
not swallow liquids without coughing and choking. The
PSW began using mouth swabs to moisten her tongue, oral
mucosa, and lips to keep her comfortable. Balm was applied
to keep her lips from cracking. The PSW invited the family to
participate in mouth care and showed them how to use the
swabs. Her family welcomed the opportunity to help provide
comfort for Irene in her last days and hours.

Nausea and Vomiting
Gathering Information
She was crying on the bed when I entered her room. “I’m so
tired of this nausea, I wish I could just die.”
Observing
I sat with her and held her hand … It was all I could think of
to help at that moment. A PSW needs to be aware that people signal they are feeling
nauseated in many ways. For example, a mildly nauseated
When she was less distressed, we talked about the things that person might say, “I don’t feel good” or “My stomach is jump-
make it worse and those things that might make it better. We ing” or “I have an upset tummy.” When nausea increases, the
made a few changes, and then I phoned the nurse to report. person may tell you, “I think I am going to throw up” or “I’m
going to be sick.”

Competency Touchstone sea if they
The PSW: • Say, “My stomach doesn’t feel good” or “I have an upset
Provides appropriate interventions as per

the care plan. Provides comfort measures for
• Burp much more frequently than usual

• Begin taking deep breaths or holding their breath for
(BC Centre for Palliative Care, 2018)
a short time
• Place pressure on their abdomen using their hands and
Nausea is a feeling of discomfort centred in a person’s stom- arms
ach and extending up to their throat. Sometimes nausea • Suddenly decrease their intake of or refuse food and
creates the feeling there is a lump at the back of the throat. drink
These feelings of discomfort can range from mild to severe. • Begin rocking or any repetitive continuous movement
Nausea is often, but not always, accompanied by vomiting, for no apparent reason
that is, the contents of the stomach and digestive system • Make retching and gagging sounds
moving in the reverse direction, coming up the esophagus
and out through the mouth. These two symptoms can be If the person vomits, observe and record the date and time
very difficult for a person to tolerate, often as difficult as they vomited and a description of what they vomited,
severe pain. including the amount, texture, and colour. Report the
vomiting to the team.
Working quickly to manage the causes and effects of nausea
and vomiting or relieving the symptoms greatly improves a
dying person’s comfort. Reducing nausea and vomiting can
be difficult because they have many causes (e.g., medica-
tion, pain, stomach irritation, brain swelling, anxiety, odours)
(BC Centre for Palliative Care, 2017). As PSWs provide most
of the personal care and support for the dying person, your
input is valuable for identifying the causes of nausea or
vomiting.

Good pain management requires a team approach, and be watchful for behaviour changes that may indicate pain.

Reflective Activity on Different Types of Pain
Pain is produced through a number of different pathways Visceral pain comes from the organs, such as the heart,
in the body, each pathway producing different sensations. intestines, or liver. The pain may be local, it may seem to
Reflect on a pain you experienced, and describe the sensa- move around, or it may refer to another area of the body.
tions. How do these pains differ? Abdominal pain is often described as cramping, colicky, and
• A mild toothache intermittent, and is often associated with constipation. The
• A muscle sprain pain comes in surges and may seem to move around.
• A broken bone
• An episode of constipation Pain associated with a sunburn, a cold sore, or shingles
• The experience of childbirth involves the nerves—neuropathic pain. When this type of
• The experience of passing a kidney stone pain occurs, the skin is more sensitive than normal. Stimu-
• A cold sore lation or touch that would not normally hurt may be really
• An episode of shingles painful. This pain is caused by an “unhappy nerve” and is
• A sunburn often a shooting pain, or the pain grabs the person instantly.
Such pain is unpredictable. Neuropathic pain may be
As you consider these pains, you may notice how different described as burning, itching, numbness, shooting, icy cold,
each one is. “pins and needles,” or like an electrical jolt. It usually follows
a distinct pattern along a nerve pathway and may be con-
Pain comes from different types of body tissue, which is why tinuous or intermittent.
each pain feels different.
It is possible to have pain that is a combination of the differ-
The pain of a broken bone and a mild toothache may both ent types of pain. For example, a person with a toothache
involve localized aching pain from the bone and muscle. might have the constant aching, throbbing originating in
The person may describe their pain as deep, aching, pene- the muscle and bone, and nerve pain causing severe sensi-
trating, or probing. If the pain is untreated, it may spread to tivity to heat, cold, or pressure.
other areas.
People may also have several different types of pain caused
by their illness or treatment for it.

My grandmother became depressed when she was dying.
It turns out that she did not like being in a back bedroom.
She wanted to be in the living room to know what was hap-
pening. She wanted to give us advice and tell us what to
do. She still wanted to make decisions. Once we put her in
the front room and she was part of the action, she stopped
being so sad.

A grieving person may find it difficult to think clearly. Other
for First Nations, Inuit, Métis and urban

Indigenous people
the course of events, trying to recall or understand what hap- • Understands cumulative grief which can

Cultural
A person’s culture may affect how they express grief.
Among people in some cultures (e.g., British or Euro-
pean), public expressions of grief have historically been dis-
couraged. For other cultures, private expression of grief may
also be discouraged. Among some Asian cultures, a stoic atti-
tude is encouraged to the extent that many grieving individ-
uals experience depression from internalizing their grief (Grief
Speaks, 2020).
At the opposite end of how grief is expressed are cultures
that do so openly. Among Haitians, cultural expressions of
grief encourage large physical displays of great emotion.

Greta’s experiences show her to be an intuitive griever. She of life. People who deny illness may report fewer common
expresses her emotions readily. In her grief, she cries often symptoms, such as fatigue, nausea and vomiting, appetite
and sometimes feels like she lacks control. She finds support loss, and pain (Vos et al., 2010).

Jack’s experiences suggest he is an instrumental griever. He
stays busy, focuses on caring for others, and appears to be
coping with daily activities. He makes meaning of his son’s
death by preparing a park bench to celebrate his life, and
working toward a public memorial garden. Jack struggles
to maintain control while he feels deep pain, turmoil, and
anxiety. He copes with the loss in the same manner that
he copes with other problems that he needs to solve. He
appreciates privacy and does not welcome questions that
address feelings, but he may accept help with his projects.
Both Greta and Jack are adjusting to the world without their
son and are grieving in different ways. Neither way is right or
wrong. Their experiences highlight different grieving styles.

Chapter 5: Providing Psychosocial Care 171

Robert’s Experience with Grieving
When Robert’s wife died, people from the church and others
that he barely knew in his neighbourhood left many casseroles
and baked goods on his doorstep. He had no appetite and felt
guilty when the food went bad.
Regina was the PSW caring for Robert. She wondered why Rob-
ert didn’t eat the food and why he was not expressing gratitude
for the meals. She resisted the urge to judge him and decided
to be curious. One day she asked him, “Robert, of all the things
that you have to deal with right now, what would you say is the
most difficult for you?”
He looked her in the eye and said, “I just miss her. I feel so awfully
lonely I just want to lie down and die.”
Regina’s heart felt like it was melting. She put her hand on his
shoulder and sat down beside him. “What do you need the
most right now?”
She was very surprised when he said, “I want some company.
I don’t want the casseroles. I want someone to sit down and
share a cup of tea with me.” Regina and Robert then talked
about how he could make that happen.

I remember a dying woman who wanted me to be with her
person visit to validate what they are seeing.
when she shared some advice with her teenage daughter.
After we had that very intimate time together, and when I
was alone with the daughter, I asked her if she had a sense of
how long her mom might live.
The daughter replied, “I used to think that she had a long time,
but now I don’t think she has very long … maybe six months.”
I was concerned when I heard this, as I figured that the mom
had only days to live. I talked with the daughter and sug-
gested that I did not think it would be that long, and that I
thought it would be a good idea for her to talk with the coun-
sellor and the doctor later that day. I was able to arrange
some supports for her.

Chapter 6: Caring in the Last Days and Hours 185

Physical Changes and Psychosocial Implications
This section discusses physical changes that often occur These are common physical changes that a person may
during the last days and hours, and the psychosocial impli- experience during the last days and hours as they are dying:
cations of the physical changes when a person is actively • Decreased physical strength and increased drowsiness
dying. Apply the information from these sections together • Reduced intake and difficulty swallowing
with that provided in Chapter 4, “Supporting Physical Com- • Delirium or confusion
fort,” to support the person and their family. • Agitation and/or restlessness
• Unresponsiveness
Know that not all dying people experience every change, • Irregular breathing
nor do they experience the changes in the order listed • Congested breathing
below. Dying people experience some or all of these chan- • Changes in skin colour and temperature
ges, in their own unique order and timing. • Muscle twitching
• Dry eyes
The family may look to you, the PSW, for your insight into • Lack of urinary output and/or bowel and bladder
what is happening. Talking with the family can validate the incontinence
changes they are seeing and may help them to acknow-

As a PSW, you can help the dying person in these ways:
• Give the person thickened fluids if directed by the
nurse or dietitian.
• Remind the person to swallow if they forget.
• Do not give anything by mouth if the person is cough-
ing or choking on fluids.

Carol had suffered a series of small strokes followed by a mas- Her family began to weep as she took her last breaths. One of
sive stroke that left her blind, mute, and partially paralyzed. Her them took up the paper Carol had written on. Barely legible
large family had gathered around her in the hospital to say their were the words, “Tell Victoria Charles says hi.”
last farewells. Carol’s death was marking the passing of a gener-
ation in their family: her brother, Charles, had died only the year
before, leaving his wife, Victoria, a widow, but consoled by their
many grandchildren.
As the physical changes of the last days and hours before death
began to occur, Carol’s eyes started to track across the room,
almost as if she could see something despite her blindness. A
smile broke across her face and she lifted her arms and acted
as if she were embracing the air above her. Her family stood
bewildered, watching this. Carol gestured for a pen and paper,
making urgent writing motions as she continued to radiate a
joyful smile. When the paper and pen were placed in her hand,
she scrawled for a few moments, then relaxed back against the
cushions, still smiling as she closed her eyes.

man who had died. The PSW began to tell the story of a won-
derful man, his fascinating life, and how he was much loved
by his devoted family. Together the nurse and the PSW talked
back and forth while they gently washed the body, changed
the sheets, cleaned and tidied the room, and threw out the
garbage. Then they stood at the bedside, hand in hand, in a
moment of silence.
The PSW and the family picked a few of the man’s much-
loved flowers from his garden and gathered together around
the bedside. In this space the family reminisced and shared
memories and stories.
The ritual of caregiving brought order out of chaos and pre-
pared a space for the family to come together to honour their
loved one.

Supporting the Family
the woman died, the deer left. The family was moved by their

PSWs can provide physical and emotional nourishment
special experience.
to family. The family of a person who has died may feel
exhausted, hungry, thirsty, tired, emotional, and/or tearful.
They may not remember what they are supposed to do
following the death and may need to be reminded. As the
PSW, you can help in these ways:
• Offer nourishment, such as a hot drink and a snack.
Companioning someone through to death can be
tiring. Physical nourishment provides the family with a
break before taking care of the body. It can also provide
space for spiritual support.
• Invite individual people or the family to sit with their
loved one. You might say things like the following:
Some people like to have some quiet time with their
loved one following the person’s death. Would you
like to have some time together as a group or some
time alone with your dad?
Some people don’t really know what they want to
do or say when they sit with their loved one after
the person has died. Some people talk as though
the person is still alive and able to hear them.
Others prefer to sit quietly in prayer or meditation.
The following story is shared by Lindsay Borrows, who writes
of the rituals in her Anishinaabe community. I can sit with you if that would help you feel more
comfortable.

Chapter 6: Caring in the Last Days and Hours 199

Immediately after a person dies, we open a window. We believe
this freshens the room; some believe it is a pathway out for the
spirit. A staff member puts a butterfly on the frame of the door
to let everyone know that the resident has died. We tidy up the
resident if needed, tidy the bed, put all bed rails down, and give
the family privacy to say their goodbyes.
Staff come to say goodbye to the family, because we are losing
them when we lose the resident.
When the family leaves, we say goodbye to our resident.
When the funeral home staff come, we place a dignity quilt over
the body bag, and a member of our staff walks the resident’s
body out to the funeral van and returns with the quilt.
We place a decorated memory box on the bed of the resident.
On the lid of the box is a beautiful poem and information on
grief counselling from one of our community partners, which
also donates the boxes to our home.
We put a sympathy card on our table in the staff room for staff
to sign and write stories about our resident. Within a week, it is
mailed to the family. It is a beautiful moment when you look
down at this card and see the many stories and signatures of
the many people who have worked hard to make sure this resi-
dent left this world with dignity and love.
— Jackie McDonald
Debriefing with staff There is no hierarchy, just co-workers remembering our
special resident. The debriefing is led by a personal sup-

This emerging field is giving rise to new types of caregivers:
death doulas and death midwives. Like the palliative care
teams, the doulas and midwives provide holistic, family- and
person- centred care for the dying and deceased. Funeral
celebrants lead services and rituals that are personalized
to the beliefs and style of the deceased people and their
families.

206 Integrating a Palliative Approach: Essentials for Personal Support Workers

Figure 1 . Checking in: Signs of compassion fatigue
Red Zone
Feeling
You feel unable to cope. If you can even fall asleep, you wake up wondering how you can get out of bed, care for the
kids, go to work, and care for those people you are assigned to for one more day.
You are not interested in hearing about any problems or advocating for anyone who needs any changes, and you hope
that no one asks anything extra of you.
Your co-workers, if they are not aware, should be aware to leave you alone and not ask for your help.
Or:
You are frequently tearful at the drop of a hat. You feel angry if someone even looks at you.
Someone suggested you take a stress leave, but you can’t imagine how much work it might take to make that happen.
You wonder if it would be easier to quit your job.
You have never been depressed before, but wonder if this is what depression looks and feels like.
Thinking
I didn’t realize I had a problem. I knew I was tired. I knew I was frustrated … But one day I woke up and I could not get out of
bed. I could not care for my kids, or go to work. I was totally and completely immobilized. My partner took me to the doctor. She
signed me off work for a month. I could not believe it. A month … surely I would feel better in a few days. Nevertheless, a month
went, and the numbness was just wearing off and the pain was just beginning. It took me nine months before I was back at
work.
Reflection
Have you been in the Red Zone? Are you in the Red Zone now? What does the Red Zone feel like for you?
If you are in the Red Zone now, speak to your doctor or counsellor. Get support from the Employee Assistance Program.
Is there anything that you can do today to nourish yourself?
Can you connect with a friend or a supervisor to talk about how you are doing?
Can you ask friends/family for support to help you get professional support and develop some strategies to get out of
the Red Zone?

Creating a social support network
Having social support in your personal life is important to
sustaining yourself. Consider talking with a partner, friend,
or roommate about the work you do, the fatigue you
experience, and what is helpful when you are feeling very
depleted. Seek out like-minded people for conversation and
friendship.
As you connect with people, listen to them the way that
you want to be heard. Be fully present when you are with
them. Develop trust by being humble and admitting your
mistakes or when you have hurt the other person.

an effective part of professional self-care. In the story “The work refreshed the next day. Leaving your worries behind
Worry Tree,” the carpenter’s ritual of “hanging his worries” on may help you to de-stress, recharge, and be able to provide
the tree before entering his home allows him to leave work excellent palliative care on your next shift (Yoder, 2010).
at work.
Other examples of rituals for creating space between work
and your home include playing a specific type of music or
piece of music as a signal to yourself that you are no longer
at work. For some people, going straight into a shower and
changing into non-work clothes is a preferred ritual. Get-
ting off the bus a stop or two before your usual stop to walk
and de-stress before arriving home can also be an effective

ritual. The important part is sending your mind a clear mes- quick fix. (I say this as I dream of my favourite dark chocolate
sage that “work is over, leave your worries behind.” Regular ice cream!)

concerns behind and to provide your mind with a break and Drinking water
time to re-energize.

About the Author
Kath is a hospice palliative care nurse, a thanatologist (which means that she specializes in death,
dying, and bereavement), an educator, a mom, and a nana.
She has a love for education that is engaging, delicious, and digestible. Her passion is to develop
and provide excellent resources (based on current research and best practice) to help front line
caregivers provide excellent care for the dying and their family.
Kath appreciates hearing about your experiences and receiving feedback. You can email Kath
from the Life and Death Matters website, www.lifeanddeathmatters.ca.
